| 2026-08-15 | [#113](https://github.com/Umbrella-IT-Group/metamcp/pull/113) | **Bound the audit write path: clamp the two caller-controlled envelope fields on every path, cap request volume ON `/trpc` ONLY, and make a dropped audit row visible.** Three fixes against one property: `audit_log` carries BEFORE UPDATE / DELETE / TRUNCATE triggers and no prune path (migration 0028), so both the SIZE and the ABSENCE of a row are permanent. **(1) Clamp.** `actor_user_agent` and `actor_ip` were the only columns outside `detail` written verbatim from a request header, on paths needing no credential (every no-credential 401 on `/metamcp/*`, every anonymous `protectedProcedure` miss on `/trpc`). Both now route through the existing `clampAuditText` helper that already guarded `detail`: 512 for the User-Agent (real agent strings run under 200 characters), 64 for the IP (the longest legitimate value is a full IPv4-mapped IPv6 address at 45 characters plus a zone id, so anything longer is not an address and truncating it costs no evidence). The IP is clamped at its stamping site in `resolveClientIp`, not per emitter, so every row and every relay-stamped `x-audit-client-ip` inherits it and a future emitter cannot forget. `auditContextFromHook` is clamped too: it reads the User-Agent straight off the caller's headers on the sign-up and sign-in paths, which are reachable without a session. **(2) `/trpc` rate limit.** That router mounted helmet, cors and the handler and nothing else, so an anonymous flood was a request-to-INSERT amplifier. New `middleware/trpc-rate-limit.middleware.ts` reuses the `AuthRateLimiter` class keyed on `req.auditClientIp` (CF-Connecting-IP), deliberately NOT on `req.ip`: this fork leaves express `trust proxy` unset, so `req.ip` is one loopback address for every caller and both existing identifiers in `lib/auth-rate-limiter` are documented as a single shared org bucket. Keying a request-rate limiter there would be a scheduled outage rather than a control. Budget 600/minute per client IP, far above real UI burst since the frontend batches via `httpBatchLink`. The no-CF-IP class (direct-to-origin, local dev) is EXEMPT rather than collapsed into one "unknown" key, because a shared bucket re-creates exactly that outage on the internal callers; the accepted cost is that a direct-to-origin attacker skips the limiter, which is a caller who has already defeated the ingress assumption every `actor_ip` rests on, and whom the `max: 2` audit pool already bounds. Mounted after cors so preflights stay out of the budget and a 429 carries the CORS headers the browser needs to read it. No audit row is emitted for a 429, which would reintroduce the amplifier one layer up. **(3) Silent loss made detectable.** `emit()` logged write failures at debug, and production runs `LOG_LEVEL=info`, whose console floor in `utils/logger` is INFO: the line reached `app.log` but never the console a responder watches, so a lost row was also an invisible one. Now a WARN, rate-limited to one line a minute, carrying a running count of rows lost since startup. The count is a TOTAL and not a per-window delta because a delta is stranded whenever the burst producing it stops before the next report fires, and a stranded count is the same silent loss. WARN and not ERROR: a dropped row is a degraded record, not a failed request, and an audit sink that pages on a database blip gets turned off. **(4) A refusal is detectable.** The 429 path writes no audit row on purpose (see above), which left the log as the only place a flood could surface, and it said nothing at all. A refused request now logs one WARN carrying a running total of refusals since startup, throttled to one line a minute in the same shape as `reportAuditWriteFailure`: first refusal reports immediately because detection must not wait out a window, and the count is a total rather than a per-window delta because a delta is stranded whenever the burst that produced it stops before the next report fires. The client IP is `JSON.stringify`'d, not interpolated raw, since it is a header value and this fork already fixed one log-forging interpolation on the mcp-proxy connect line. **Review pass (fix-first), added after the first round.** Two of the three fixes were correct but had no test that could fail if they were removed: the `auditContextFromHook` clamps could be deleted with the suite still green, and `trpcRouter.use(trpcRateLimitMiddleware)` could be replaced by a bare reference to the export while staying green AND typecheck-clean, i.e. `/trpc` would silently go back to having no limiter. Both now have biting tests. The mount is pinned by reading `routers/trpc.ts` as source (mounting the real router drags the whole procedure tree and the MCP pool into the import graph), the same technique as the repo-wide `cors()` drift guard in `routers/cors-policy.test.ts`, and it pins the ORDER too: after cors so preflights stay out of the budget, before the tRPC handler because a request refused afterwards has already written the rows the limiter exists to bound. Also from review: the `trpc:` key prefix is documented as descriptive rather than load-bearing (isolation comes from `trpcRateLimiter` being its own `AuthRateLimiter` instance with its own Map), and the sibling cleanup sweep in `lib/auth-rate-limiter` gained the `.unref()` its neighbour already had, since importing that module registered a 10-minute timer that held the event loop open for every consumer. Test evidence: 1339 passed / 22 skipped / 0 failed on this branch, against 1328 / 22 / 0 at the pre-review tip and a clean-checkout baseline of 1313 / 22 / 0. The first round mutation-checked its three fixes (removing either clamp, keying the limiter on `req.ip`, reverting WARN to debug). This round mutation-checked four more: delete both `auditContextFromHook` clamps, 2 red; replace the mount with `void trpcRateLimitMiddleware`, 2 red and `tsc --noEmit` still clean, which is the point; delete the `reportRefusal` call, 3 red; drop `.unref()` from the shared sweep, 1 red. **REMAINING, stated so the headline is not read wider than it is:** the row-COUNT bound exists on `/trpc` and nowhere else. `/metamcp/*` mounts `authenticateApiKey` ahead of `rateLimitMiddleware` (`routers/public-metamcp/sse.ts`, `routers/public-metamcp/streamable-http.ts`), so every no-credential 401 writes its `mcp.auth.denied` row BEFORE any limiter is consulted, and that limiter is per-endpoint opt-in (`enable_max_rate` / `enable_client_max_rate`) and off by default: an anonymous caller still gets one INSERT per request there. `/oauth/register` writes one `oauth.dcr.register` row per anonymous registration behind `rateLimitRegistration` (30 a minute keyed on `CF-Connecting-IP` through `resolveClientIp`, its own limiter instance since [#110](https://github.com/Umbrella-IT-Group/metamcp/pull/110) split it off the token endpoint's bucket), so that surface is already keyed per caller rather than per organisation. *(This sentence originally named `rateLimitToken` and the shared `req.ip` bucket; corrected 2026-08-17 against `routers/oauth/registration.ts` + `routers/oauth/utils.ts`, where the claim had been stale since #110.)* **The keying half of this follow-on lane is CLOSED by [#122](https://github.com/Umbrella-IT-Group/metamcp/pull/122) (2026-08-17):** `getAuthRateLimitIdentifier`, the failed-auth limiter the `/metamcp/*` path consults in `middleware/api-key-oauth.middleware`, now resolves the caller through the shared `lib/client-ip` helper, so neither surface named in this paragraph still sits on the shared `req.ip` bucket. | 2026-08-15 | [#112](https://github.com/Umbrella-IT-Group/metamcp/pull/112) | **Fail-closed registration defaults, an explicit bootstrap exemption so the closed default cannot lock the gateway out of its own administrator, and an audit row for boot-time registration flips.** `parseEnvConfig` defaulted both `BOOTSTRAP_DISABLE_REGISTRATION_UI` and `BOOTSTRAP_DISABLE_REGISTRATION_SSO` to `false`, matching upstream, so a template-derived deploy that never set the variables silently ran with self-registration OPEN, an unauthenticated account-creation exposure on a gateway whose access model assumes accounts are provisioned. Both defaults are now `true`: absence is read as disabled, and since `parseBool` returns its default for an unparseable value as well as an undefined one, a typo (`flase`) also fails closed instead of opening the door. **What makes that default SAFE is a scoped exemption for bootstrap's own account creation, not ordering.** A deploy onboards its administrators through `BOOTSTRAP_USERS`, and `ensureUser` does that by POSTing to Better Auth's own `/api/auth/sign-up/email` through `auth.handler`, which runs `databaseHooks.user.create.before` in `auth.ts`, which THROWS while `DISABLE_SIGNUP` is stored `true`. An earlier revision of this change tried to solve that by ORDERING alone, moving the two control writes to after `bootstrapUsers`. That is not sufficient, and the reasoning behind it was wrong in a way worth recording: the control writes PERSIST, so the flag is already stored `true` at the top of every boot after the first, and no ordering inside a single boot can undo what a previous boot wrote. The failure that exposes it is an ORDINARY CONTAINER RESTART with `BOOTSTRAP_RECREATE_USER=true` (the value `example.env` ships): `ensureUser` DELETES the existing administrator, and its user-scoped API keys with it, BEFORE the re-signup, so the refusal that follows leaves the deploy with no administrator, registration closed, and the connector keys unrecoverable. The fix is a new module-scope switch, `apps/backend/src/lib/bootstrap-signup-override.ts` (`setBootstrapSignupAllowed` / `isBootstrapSignupAllowed`, default `false`), consulted by BOTH branches of the `user.create.before` guard, which now refuses only when the stored flag is set AND the exemption is shut. Both branches rather than only the basic-auth one because `isSsoRegistration` there is a path heuristic, not a fact about the caller, and bootstrap's success must not depend on how better-auth happens to label the request. `initializeEnvironmentConfiguration` is the switch's only writer: it opens the exemption immediately before the bootstrap user pass and closes it in a `finally` immediately after, so a throw inside that pass cannot leave it on. The window is not reachable from outside the process: the whole sequence runs inside `initializeOnStartup()`, which `index.ts` awaits BEFORE `app.listen()`, so the HTTP server is not accepting connections for any part of it and every request that ever arrives meets the closed gate. The exemption is also not an audit hole, because `user.create.after` still emits `emitSignupCreated` for the account, so a bootstrap-created administrator is exactly as visible in `audit_log` as any other. The ordering change is KEPT, demoted to what it actually is: a cheap second line that stops the stored flag from moving until this boot's accounts exist, so a future change that drops or misplaces the exemption degrades to a failed first boot rather than to a delete-then-refuse on an established deploy. `applyRegistrationControls` is still called in the `shouldSkipBootstrap` early-return path too, so a guarded `BOOTSTRAP_ONLY_FIRST_RUN` boot asserts both flags; that path creates no users and so never opens the exemption. Note `validateConfig`'s existing lockout warning does NOT cover any of this: it fires only when `config.users.length === 0`, and the dangerous combination is disabled-WITH-users-configured, where users exist to be created and the refusal is silent. The lock is structural for a BOOTSTRAP-ENABLED deploy, not universally: a `BOOTSTRAP_ENABLE=false` deploy never reaches this entrypoint (`startup.ts`), writes no config row, and the `configService` readers treat a missing row as `false`, so it keeps upstream's open-by-absent-row behaviour and has to be closed by hand in the admin UI. This is a deliberate divergence from upstream and is called out on both `example.env` lines ("Default: true (Umbrella fork; upstream defaults false)"), with the file's `BOOTSTRAP_DISABLE_REGISTRATION_UI` value flipped `false` to `true`; `README.md` stopped claiming registration controls are "unchanged" from upstream, gained a fork-knobs table for the two variables, describes the bootstrap exemption and why it opens no reachable window, and the quick-start `cp example.env .env` step now warns that with registration closed the bootstrap account is the only way in, so the placeholder `test@user.example` / `changeme` pair has to be replaced before the first `docker compose up`. Second half, unchanged from the previous revision: the two boot-time `upsertConfig` writes that apply these controls (`DISABLE_SIGNUP`, `DISABLE_SSO_SIGNUP`) were bare, with no read-back and no audit row, so a container restart carrying a changed (or newly absent) variable could move who may create an account and leave nothing behind but the config row's `updated_at`. Each write now reads the current value first and, only when it actually changed, emits the SAME per-key action the admin UI setter emits (`config.signup_disabled.set` / `config.sso_signup_disabled.set`, `target_type: "config_key"`, `target_id` the config key, `detail: {old_value, new_value, source: "bootstrap_env"}` with the values as booleans), so a bootstrap flip and an administrator's flip read as one timeline in `audit_log` instead of an audit table that has to be correlated against container logs. `emitAdminEvent(undefined, ...)` stamps `actor_type: "system"`, which is the truth: no administrator is behind a restart. The emitter is sync, void and self-catching, so it is never awaited and cannot fail the boot, and it fires strictly AFTER the write (a row claiming a flag moved, from a boot whose write then threw, is worse than no row). A failed read yields `old_value: null`, which compares unequal to any boolean and so assumes changed: over-reporting one redundant row after a database blip beats losing the row that says registration reopened. That catch also logs a warning, because the row it produces is degraded (null `old_value`, emitted even if nothing moved) and the log line is what tells an investigator the null is a failed read rather than a state the flag was ever in. Change-only emission matters because bootstrap re-asserts both flags on EVERY start, so unconditional rows would bury the one restart that moved a flag. Tests, 13 new cases across two files. `bootstrap.order.test.ts` gains a `storedSignupDisabled` fixture that makes the stand-in `auth.handler` refuse exactly as the real gate does (that suite mocks `../auth` wholesale, so it can only see bootstrap's half), an `authCallLog` field recording whether the exemption was open at each sign-up POST, and 5 cases: an administrator IS recreated on a boot where `DISABLE_SIGNUP` is already stored `true` and the recreate delete has already run, with the preserved key restored carrying its original secret and the fresh ids and the boot still leaving registration closed; the exemption is shut after a normal pass; the exemption is shut after the user pass THROWS; the exemption is shut even when the CATCH'S OWN logging throws and the boot fails outright, which is the case that makes `finally` load-bearing rather than decorative; and a guarded `BOOTSTRAP_ONLY_FIRST_RUN` boot never opens it. Two mutations initially did NOT bite and both are recorded because the fix was to the TESTS, not to the claim. The default-flag mutation was masked by the suite's own `afterEach`, which writes the flag before that case could observe its initial value; the case was rewritten around `vi.resetModules()` and re-verified. Moving the clear to after the whole try/catch was survived because today's catch swallows, which makes that placement genuinely equivalent for the `bootstrapUsers`-throws path; rather than assert a difference that does not exist, a sixth case was added for the path where it DOES (the catch's own logging failing), and the code comment was corrected to claim only that. | 2026-08-15 | [#110](https://github.com/Umbrella-IT-Group/metamcp/pull/110) | **OAuth surface hardening, MERGED (squash `deae38b`).** Backfilled close-out for the `sec/b-oauth-surface-hardening` (PR pending) row above, which landed on `umbrella` without ever being reconciled to its PR number. Six changes on the anonymous OAuth surface, with no change to the connector pairing contract. **(1) DCR input caps.** `buildClientRegistration` bounded nothing it stored; `redirect_uris` now caps at 10 entries of 512 characters, `client_name` at 255, the four metadata URIs at 512, `contacts` at 10 entries of 255, `software_id` at 255 and `software_version` at 64, with non-strings refused rather than cast, all through the existing RFC 7591 error pair so the wire contract is unchanged. **(2) Body limit decoupled.** `/oauth/*` rode the app-wide `express.json({limit:"50mb"})` registered ahead of the router, so the 10mb the router asked for never bound anything (body-parser marks a request read and later parsers no-op); the global parser now skips OAuth-served paths and the router's own parser binds at 256kb ahead of the rate limiter, with the branches moved into `lib/global-body-parser.ts` so a test can mount the real wiring instead of a hand-copied model app. **(3) Never-used-DCR retention sweep**, on the existing 5-minute cleanup interval, gated on provenance because admin-minted and DCR-minted client rows were byte-identical: migration `0029_oauth_client_registration_source` adds `oauth_clients.registration_source` (`'dcr'` \| `'admin'`, plus a CHECK constraint and a `$type` narrowing), each mint path stamps its own value, and only `'dcr'` is swept. Nullable with no default in both fail-safe directions, so rows predating 0029 and any future insert path that forgets to stamp one are kept rather than deleted. **(4) Registration rate bucket split**, giving `/oauth/register` its own 30/min bucket keyed on `CF-Connecting-IP` so anonymous registrations cannot 429 a paired connector's token exchange. **(5) Authorize-time redirect allowlist**, running `isAllowedRedirectUri` at `/oauth/authorize` in addition to (not in place of) the existing `validateRedirectUri`, so a pre-existing or off-allowlist client row cannot send a code to an attacker host. **(6) Introspection gated**, since `POST /oauth/introspect` took no credential contrary to RFC 7662 §2.1 and was an active-token oracle returning `scope`, `client_id` and `sub`; it now requires a first-party MetaMCP API key. `/oauth/revoke` deliberately stays public and the reason is recorded at the route itself: revocation DESTROYS a credential and cannot be reached without already holding the token value, and these are secretless public PKCE clients with nothing to authenticate with, so gating it would break revocation for the clients least able to protect a token. | `apps/backend/src/routers/oauth/{client-registration,client-retention,introspection-auth,registration,authorization,token,utils,index}.ts`, `apps/backend/src/lib/global-body-parser.ts`, `apps/backend/src/db/repositories/oauth.repo.ts`, `apps/backend/src/db/schema.ts`, `apps/backend/src/trpc/oauth-clients.impl.ts`, `apps/backend/src/index.ts`, `apps/backend/drizzle/0029_oauth_client_registration_source.sql`, `apps/backend/drizzle/meta/_journal.json`, `packages/zod-types/src/oauth.zod.ts` | Yes for the input caps, the body limit and the introspection gate, which apply to upstream unchanged. | 2026-08-15 | [#116](https://github.com/Umbrella-IT-Group/metamcp/pull/116) | **Harden the anonymous /metamcp surface: fail-close accidentally-unauthenticated endpoints, remove an endpoint-name enumeration oracle, and stop shipping usable example credentials.** None of the three was exploitable on the deployed estate; all three are the kind of thing an attacker who can read this source goes looking for. **(1) CONDITION 1 now fails closed.** `authenticateApiKey` answered an endpoint with BOTH `enable_api_key_auth` and `enable_oauth` off with `return next(); // Pass through without authentication`, so a single mis-set pair of checkboxes published that namespace's entire tool set to any anonymous caller, with no credential to steal, no audit actor to name and no rate-limit bucket to fill. It is now refused with the ordinary 401 challenge unless the deployment sets `ALLOW_UNAUTHENTICATED_ENDPOINTS=true` (strict string compare, so `1` / `yes` / `TRUE` do not open it), and the refusal logs a warning naming the endpoint, deduped per endpoint uuid because the refused endpoint is one whose request rate the attacker sets. A request carrying no endpoint record at all fails closed regardless of the flag: that is a routing bug, not an operator's choice. Every refusal also emits the existing `mcp.auth.denied` audit row, reason `unauthenticated_endpoint_refused`. **(2) The enumeration oracle is closed and the auth-mode fingerprint removed.** `lookupEndpoint` resolves the URL name before authentication (it must, since `authenticateApiKey` reads `req.endpoint` to pick an auth mode), and answered an unknown name with `404 No endpoint found with name: <name>` while a real name continued to a 401. That is a free, uncredentialed, unlimited does-this-exist answer, and the 401 that followed named the endpoint's auth mode on top of it: `supported_methods` listed exactly which of `X-API-Key`, `?api_key=` and Bearer the endpoint accepted, and `error_description` said which. A wordlist plus those two facts is a map of the estate and of its weakest doors. Unknown names now get byte-identically what an unauthenticated request to a real OAuth-enabled endpoint gets, header included, and all three no-credential paths share one body with no mode detail in it. The real reason is kept in the server log, JSON-stringified so an attacker-supplied name cannot forge log lines. A new per-client-IP limiter (20 not-found probes per 5 minutes, its own `AuthRateLimiter` instance, keyed on `CF-Connecting-IP` via `req.auditClientIp` and exempting the no-header class exactly as `trpc-rate-limit.middleware` documents) bounds a probe run before the database is touched; once tripped it refuses NAME-INDEPENDENTLY, real endpoints included, because a limiter that only refused not-founds would rebuild the same oracle out of 429-versus-401. **The same oracle existed on `/metamcp/<name>/admin/*`**, where `lookupEndpoint` ran ahead of the session gate so an anonymous caller got 404-versus-401 there too. None of the three gate middlewares reads `req.endpoint`, and neither do the two handlers, so the lookup moved BEHIND the gate in a new `afterAuthentication` form that keeps the honest 404 for the authenticated administrator who is the only caller who should be able to tell the difference. `GET /metamcp/` was checked and needed nothing: it has been admin-gated since the estate-gate change and already withholds the endpoint list from anonymous callers. **(3) example.env ships no usable credential.** `BOOTSTRAP_USER_PASSWORD=changeme` (an ADMINISTRATOR account, and the first password anyone who has read this repository would try) and `POSTGRES_PASSWORD=m3t4mcp` become `REPLACE_ME__generate_a_strong_password`, and the backend now logs a loud, unmissable warning at bootstrap if either literal reaches a real boot. A warning rather than a refusal on purpose: the account is created at boot, so hard-failing would brick a deliberate throwaway dev stack. CONNECTOR SAFETY: the `WWW-Authenticate` challenge and `resource_metadata` are unchanged byte for byte, so the OAuth discovery handshake every MCP connector performs on its first request behaves exactly as before; only prose and the unread `supported_methods` array were dropped, and nothing in this repo or the frontend consumed either. A correct, authenticated connector request is unaffected on every path. TWO ACCEPTED COSTS, recorded rather than hidden. An endpoint with `enable_oauth` OFF still answers without the `WWW-Authenticate` header, so an API-KEY-ONLY endpoint stays distinguishable from a non-existent name by that header's absence; closing it would mean advertising OAuth on an endpoint that rejects OAuth tokens, which is the MCP-inspector retry loop that header's absence exists to prevent, and it cannot be settled without live connector testing. And a genuinely mistyped endpoint URL now says "authenticate" instead of "no such endpoint", which moves typo diagnosis to the server log and the admin UI. 1496 backend tests green, baseline 1466; all five behaviour changes mutation-checked. | `apps/backend/src/middleware/{api-key-oauth.middleware,lookup-endpoint-middleware,endpoint-exposure.test}.ts`, `apps/backend/src/lib/{auth-challenge,bootstrap.service,bootstrap-placeholder-password.test}.ts`, `apps/backend/src/routers/public-metamcp/{admin,admin.admin-gate.test}.ts`, `example.env`, `turbo.json` | Yes, all three apply to upstream unchanged. The unauthenticated-endpoint gate is a behaviour change upstream would need to opt into deliberately, since upstream's own `example.env` bootstraps a both-toggles-off endpoint. |

| 2026-08-17 | [#120](https://github.com/Umbrella-IT-Group/metamcp/pull/120) | **Loopback redirect URIs are accepted in every environment, so an RFC 8252 native-app callback no longer depends on `NODE_ENV`.** `validateRedirectUri` (`routers/oauth/utils.ts`) refused plain-http and loopback redirects whenever `NODE_ENV === "production"`. RFC 8252 section 7.3 has an installed client receive its authorization code on `http://127.0.0.1:<ephemeral>`, plain http on a port the OS assigns at runtime, so that refusal removed the only redirect shape such a client can offer while hardening nothing reachable from the network: the loopback interface is not routable off-box. Every other production rule is byte-identical (HTTPS required for non-loopback hosts, RFC 1918 hosts refused, non-http(s) schemes refused), and the registration-time sibling `isAllowedRedirectUri` is untouched, so both checkers still run in sequence at `/oauth/authorize` and a redirect URI reaches a minted code only if both accept it. Retires a dead branch on the way past: the old `hostname === "::1"` test could never fire, because the WHATWG parser reports that host bracketed. **A second, latent defect in the same function is fixed with it:** the optional `allowedHosts` narrowing compared entries against the RAW parsed hostname while the loopback test compared a normalised one, so `["::1"]` never matched `http://[::1]/` and an entry differing in case or whitespace never matched anything. Entries are now trimmed, lowercased and bracket-stripped, matching what the registration-time checker does with its own allowlist. **Blast radius, stated because this is live rather than latent for the documented install path:** `example.env` sets `NODE_ENV=production` on its first line, `docker-compose.yml` reads the resulting `.env` through `env_file`, and the README quickstart is `cp example.env .env` followed by `docker compose up -d`, so a quickstart deployment is already in the production branch and this un-breaks loopback OAuth on it. A deployment that leaves `NODE_ENV` unset (the production `Dockerfile` sets none of its own) sees no observable change until that flips, at which point this is what keeps loopback OAuth working across the transition. Nothing that was refused before becomes acceptable in either case. **Coverage, since the function had none and a rule that only takes effect under `NODE_ENV=production` was observable only by deploying:** new `validate-redirect-uri.test.ts`, 19 tests in four blocks with `NODE_ENV` stubbed per case: loopback under production (the three RFC 8252 spellings, arbitrary ephemeral ports); what production still refuses; the `allowedHosts` narrowing, including the empty-array case pinned as "no restriction", which deliberately differs from `resolveDcrAllowedHosts` where an empty value means loopback-only, so the divergence is recorded in a test rather than left to be discovered; and **the composed authorize gate**, both checkers asserted in sequence, so the loopback accept is proven against the gate a caller actually meets rather than against one function in isolation. Five of the 19 are recorded red against the pre-change function. | `apps/backend/src/routers/oauth/{utils,validate-redirect-uri.test}.ts` | Yes. | 2026-08-17 | [#122](https://github.com/Umbrella-IT-Group/metamcp/pull/122) | **The failed-authentication rate limiter keys on the edge client IP instead of `req.ip`, so it bounds a brute force instead of handing any single caller an endpoint-wide lockout.** `getAuthRateLimitIdentifier` built `${req.ip}:${endpointId}`. The test file pins that by driving the production pair rather than the counter directly. It is documented rather than fixed because changing which method the middleware calls would change ENFORCEMENT, a separate decision from re-keying and deliberately not made in this change. *(That deferred decision was taken in #133 on 2026-08-18: the three call sites now ask the read-only `isCurrentlyLimited` BEFORE recording, so a failed request costs one count and refusal arrives on the twenty-first failure rather than the eleventh. Every number in this paragraph describes the 2026-08-17 state only.)* **Residual, in two parts.** Per-IP keying bounds a source address, not a distributed caller, which is the same trade the two sibling fixes took; what it buys is that the control can no longer be turned into the outage. Decisions worth naming, each also recorded next to the code: all six NULLABLE, because the audit write is fire-and-forget and its failure is swallowed by design, so a `NOT NULL` would not surface as an error, it would silently drop the row for every unauthenticated or passthrough call, and a partly-attributed row is worth more than no row. Parallel `tools/call` on one session is the normal case for a busy consumer, so a second request can overwrite the first's binding before it is audited (and the `request_id` meant to join a tool call to the same request's `audit_log` rows then points at a sibling); and the in-memory session lookup resolves a session on namespace plus endpoint without re-deriving the caller from the credential presented on the current request. The admin Inspector surface likewise wrote un-attributed rows, and now records the better-auth session actor under `auth_method = 'session'`, with no consumer label invented since there is no consumer there. **(4) MUST NOT REGRESS, and read this before comparing tool error rates across this deploy: the `success` column's SEMANTICS CHANGED.** An MCP tool failure is a RESULT, not a throw. A test pins the writer's allow-list against the read contract's enum so the two cannot drift. **Immutability window.** `BEFORE UPDATE` and `BEFORE TRUNCATE` triggers raise unconditionally at any row age; `BEFORE DELETE` raises unless the row is older than 30 days. These writes fire on the connect retry loop and on every backend stderr line, so a crash-looping backend is a write firehose: it must not contend for the connections the request path needs, and it must not share a budget with `audit_log` either, since a dropped security-audit row has no prune path and cannot be reconstructed. Each writer can only starve itself. Drops are reported as rate-limited WARNs carrying a running total, the same discipline `lib/audit/audit-emitter` uses. Two placement decisions: wired inside `record()` rather than at each emitter, so every present and future call site is durable without anyone opting in; and ordered LAST inside `record()` and guarded, last so the ring push, the stdout mirror and the listener fan-out are never conditional on a database, guarded because ordering alone protects only what runs before the call, and `record()` is invoked from transport `onclose`/`onerror` handlers that fire the reconnect callback afterwards, where a throw escaping it could stop a backend being reconnected. The static module graph stays database-free through a lazy repository import, so the many unit suites that reach the log store keep booting without a database; for the same reason the repository is deliberately not re-exported from `db/repositories/index.ts`. **Retention.** `GATEWAY_EVENTS_RETENTION_DAYS` (default 90) prunes on the existing cleanup interval, floor-clamped to 30 with a boot warning, because the database refuses deletions inside the window and an application that asked for one would just raise on a timer. Unlike `TOOL_AUDIT_RETENTION_DAYS` there is no keep-forever value here; zero and negatives clamp to the floor, this table being the high-volume one. The prune computes its cutoff in SQL (`now() - make_interval(days => $1)`) rather than from `Date.now()`, so it reads the same clock the trigger does and the two predicates cannot disagree at the boundary. **Read surface.** `logs.history` is an `adminProcedure`, matching `logs.get` beside it: both render the same events, so gating the durable one any lower would make it a way around the gate on the live one. Filters on time range, category, level, server, client and message substring, with LIKE metacharacters escaped and an explicit `ESCAPE` so a search for `100%` stays a search for `100%`. Paging is keyset on `(occurred_at, uuid)` fetching one extra row to decide whether a next page exists, because a timestamp alone cannot page correctly when a reconnect storm writes many rows inside one millisecond; a cursor without a pinned `from` is refused, the default window being computed from the current clock and sliding forward between requests, which would drop the oldest rows of a paging run. The server-name filter list is computed on the first page only, being a `SELECT DISTINCT` over the window that no index can serve. Retention would not merely become shorter than asked, it would stop working altogether behind an error logged every five minutes while the table grew without bound. A value of 1 to 29 is therefore raised to 30 with a WARN at boot rather than honoured or rejected: refusing to boot over a log-retention setting is a worse failure than keeping more history than asked, and honouring it silently would let a typo delete the guarantee. `<= 0` keeps its long-standing meaning of retain-forever, checked BEFORE the floor so it is never mistaken for an under-range value and raised to 30, which would start deleting rows on a deployment that asked for none to be deleted. That is a deliberate asymmetry with `GATEWAY_EVENTS_RETENTION_DAYS`, where `<= 0` clamps like any other under-range value: that table is high-volume operational history where keep-forever is an unbounded-growth setting, this one is the audit trail at one row per tool call, where keeping MORE is the safe direction. The raw value is shape-checked before parsing, because `Number.parseInt` is not a validator: it reads a leading integer and discards the rest, so `30abc` becomes 30 and `1e9` becomes 1, a typo taking effect as a number nobody wrote. **better-auth's rate limiter pinned explicitly off (`rateLimit: { enabled: false }`).** It defaults to `enabled ?? isProduction`, so it was off only because `NODE_ENV` happened to be unset; under `NODE_ENV=production` it would silently enable an in-memory limiter keyed on ip plus path, resolving the address from `x-forwarded-for` only, and with no `trustedProxies` configured it accepts that header only when it carries exactly one entry. Behind this deployment's proxy chain it carries more, the address resolves to null, and the limiter falls back to the literal key `no-trusted-ip`: ONE shared bucket per path for every caller. Worse than the headline 100-per-10s suggests, since better-auth applies a special rule of 3 per 10s to `/sign-in*`, `/sign-up*`, `/change-password*` and `/change-email*`, so any single caller could lock everyone else out of signing in. That is the same inversion this fork's own failed-auth limiter had, and pinning it off makes the pending `NODE_ENV` change behaviour-neutral on the auth surface. **The honest gap next to it, which matters more than the fix:** this pin does NOT add rate limiting to `/api/auth`. That surface is served by `routers/auth-relay.ts` calling `auth.handler` directly with no limiter mounted on it, so `/api/auth/sign-in` is unlimited today and stays unlimited after this line. The fork's own limiters cover other surfaces and not this one (`lib/auth-rate-limiter` on the lookup-endpoint, token and api-key-oauth paths, `routers/oauth/utils` on `/oauth/*`, `middleware/trpc-rate-limit.middleware` on `/trpc`). This is the SESSION SIGNING KEY: anyone holding it can mint a valid session cookie for any account without ever seeing a password, and it also keys the OAuth consent-request signatures. So a deployment that never set the variable was not merely using a weak credential, it was authenticating against a value anyone who has read this repository already knows. All four compose files move to the required form, one occurrence each, refusing to render and naming the variable rather than falling back; the devcontainer message names `.devcontainer/.env` for the same interpolation-directory reason recorded in the `POSTGRES_PASSWORD` row, verified rather than assumed. `example.env` now ships its own distinct obvious placeholder with a comment saying what the value is and how to generate one (`openssl rand -hex 32`), and a repository-wide sweep confirms those were the only occurrences of the old literal. **Rotation, which is where this differs from `POSTGRES_PASSWORD`:** there is no initdb coupling, so an existing deployment can change the value at any time with no database work. Doing so invalidates every session and consent request signed with the old key, which signs everyone out ONCE. The README quick start says this and notes the variable is required. **Boot placeholder warning, added after the first round rather than deferred.** `auth.ts` throws when the variable is unset but said nothing when it held the shipped placeholder, so a quickstart deployment that never edited the line still booted and signed sessions with a value published here. `warnOnPlaceholderAuthSecret` in `bootstrap.service.ts` now does for the signing key exactly what `warnOnPlaceholderBootstrapPassword` already did for the bootstrap password: one loud WARN at boot naming the consequence and the `openssl rand -hex 32` remedy. **Regression pin covers the fallback SYNTAX, not only the literal**, `:-` being the actual hazard since it means "use the published literal when the operator set nothing": the test asserts no `BETTER_AUTH_SECRET:-` and a present `BETTER_AUTH_SECRET:?` in all four files, so a rename cannot slip a new weak default back in. Mutation-verified: restoring the `:-` fallback in `docker-compose.test.yml` turns that assertion red, and reverting turns it green again. Verification is `docker compose config` per file, exiting 1 with the intended message when unset and rendering with zero occurrences of the old literal when set; the devcontainer file renders only once `.devcontainer/.env` exists and not from the root `.env` alone, which confirms its message points at the right path. | `docker-compose.yml`, `docker-compose.dev.yml`, `docker-compose.test.yml`, `.devcontainer/docker-compose.yml`, `example.env`, `README.md`, `apps/backend/src/lib/{bootstrap.service,bootstrap-placeholder-password.test}.ts` | Yes. | 2026-08-18 | [#129](https://github.com/Umbrella-IT-Group/metamcp/pull/129) | **Audit-table storage growth tripwire** — a periodic size check on the three audit tables (`audit_log`, `gateway_events`, `tool_call_audit`), riding the existing 5-minute cleanup sweep. `gateway_events` (migration `0031`, [#125](https://github.com/Umbrella-IT-Group/metamcp/pull/125)) and `tool_call_audit` (migration `0032`, [#126](https://github.com/Umbrella-IT-Group/metamcp/pull/126)) are immutable for their first 30 days at the database level, so nothing in the application — including the retention sweepers — can reclaim in-window space, and lowering a retention variable does nothing until the window passes; early notice is therefore the only defence that acts in time. Deliberately not a write-side rate limiter: capping writes would mean choosing at write time which security records to drop, and a dropped record is unrecoverable in exactly the tables that exist to be complete. **The query** is one statement returning three rows, using `pg_class.reltuples` and `pg_total_relation_size` rather than `count(*)` (whose cost would grow with the problem it watches for); table names sit on the left of the join so an unresolved name still returns a null-figure row, and a never-analyzed relation's `reltuples` of `-1` reports as `unknown` rather than zero. **Signal path:** every check logs one flat `key=value` INFO line per table — a heartbeat distinguishing a silent tripwire from a healthy estate; crossing `AUDIT_STORAGE_WARN_MB` (default 2048 MB) escalates the same reading through `metamcpLogStore.record()`, mirroring a WARN to stdout and persisting a `system` row to `gateway_events`, so the warning lands in the History view the operator already reads. Hysteresis caps it at one warning per table per 24h, re-arming below 90% of the threshold or on process restart. **Failure isolation:** the stats read rides the bounded two-connection `gatewayEventsDb` pool, not the main pool (unbounded queueing) and not the audit pool (no write on this path should compete with an audit insert); errors are caught and logged, and a failure never resets the check cadence. **Config:** `AUDIT_STORAGE_CHECK_INTERVAL_SWEEPS` (default `12`, hourly; floor `1`, cap `288`/24h, both boot-warned — deliberately no value that disables the check) and `AUDIT_STORAGE_WARN_MB` (default `2048`). A same-PR review follow-up closed three gaps found after the first pass: the pool's 1s timeout bounds acquiring a connection, not holding one, so `pg_total_relation_size` blocking behind a held `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE` lock (a migration, `VACUUM FULL`, the README's own break-glass `DISABLE TRIGGER`) could pin a connection indefinitely — fixed with `statement_timeout=5000` on the pool; the heartbeat's reach was overstated (the INFO line only reaches stdout when `LOG_LEVEL=info`, true of this fork's own deployment but not the default) and is now documented rather than changed; `AUDIT_STORAGE_WARN_MB` gained a 1 TiB ceiling so an unreachable threshold can't mute the escalation; and the integration suite's assertions were rescoped to rows newer than the run's start (database clock), since its own audit rows sit inside the 30-day window and can't be cleaned up between runs. **Tests**, counted on the merged tree rather than on the branch's first pass — the review follow-up above added six cases, so any figure taken from the original PR body understates them: `tripwire.test.ts` (30 cases: env parsing, interval gating, heartbeat, threshold crossing, hysteresis in all four directions, failure isolation) and `tripwire-wiring.test.ts` (5 cases, pinning the call site inside `routers/oauth/index.ts` running after the retention sweeps) run in CI; `audit-storage.integration.test.ts` (7) and `tripwire.integration.test.ts` (2) run against real Postgres behind `TEST_DATABASE_URL`. Suite totals at this commit: CI shape (no database) 120 passed/6 skipped files, 1851 passed/93 skipped tests; full backend suite against real Postgres 126 files / 1944 tests passed, measured with `--no-file-parallelism` — the postgres integration files share one database and interfere with each other under vitest's default parallelism, so a parallel run against a real database is not a reproducible number. Frontend 3/3 files and 28/28 tests; `pnpm check-types` green across all three packages; backend eslint unchanged at the 21 pre-existing warnings. | 2026-08-18 | [#133](https://github.com/Umbrella-IT-Group/metamcp/pull/133) | **A failed authentication costs one count instead of two, so the failed-auth limiter enforces the twenty its constructor asks for.** All three failed-credential branches in `middleware/api-key-oauth.middleware` ran `recordFailedAttempt(id)` and then asked `isRateLimited(id)`. `isRateLimited` is the MUTATING accessor — it counts the question it is asked, which is precisely the hazard the sibling `isCurrentlyLimited` was added to avoid and documents in its own docblock — so two counts landed per failed request. `new AuthRateLimiter(20, 1 * 60 * 1000)` therefore refused on the ELEVENTH failure in a window rather than the twenty-first: a nominal twenty enforced as roughly ten a minute per (caller, endpoint). [#122](https://github.com/Umbrella-IT-Group/metamcp/pull/122) wrote that gap down rather than closing it, on the grounds that closing it changes ENFORCEMENT while that change's subject was the limiter's KEY; this is the deferred decision, taken. The three sites now ask the read-only `isCurrentlyLimited` first and record only on the way past, so one failed request moves the counter once and the constructor argument is the enforced allowance: the twentieth failure is answered as a normal 401 and the twenty-first is refused with a 429 (`record.count >= this.maxAttempts`). **The allowance is raised rather than the constructor lowered to 10, and that is the decision rather than an incidental consequence:** ten was never chosen, it was an artifact of the call pair, and lowering the constructor to preserve it would freeze an accident as if it were a bound someone reasoned about. Twenty failed credentials a minute from one source address is what this limiter was written with, and it is still orders of magnitude below what a brute force needs. **One behavioural change beyond the allowance, named because it runs toward the caller:** a request refused with a 429 no longer adds to the count, having never reached the credential check. Nothing depended on that growth — `recordFailedAttempt` inside an open window increments the count without touching `resetTime`, so it never extended the window — and the effect is that a caller over budget stays refused for exactly the window it opened rather than one a continuing flood keeps pushing out. **Tests.** The budget block in `lib/auth-rate-limiter.test.ts` already drove the production call pair rather than the counter, which is why it read the old allowance correctly; it is rewritten to the new pair and now pins BOTH edges — nineteen failures leave the caller inside its budget, the twentieth is served, the twenty-first is refused — because an assertion that only checks "refuses eventually" cannot see an off-by-one, which is how the double count survived in the first place. A new case pins the read-only property: a caller already over budget stays refused across fifty further checks and none of them move anything. `middleware/api-key-oauth-audit.test.ts` stopped overshooting the boundary — it fired 21 attempts to land "comfortably past" an allowance it described as about ten — and now asserts the exact edge, that the twentieth attempt writes `mcp.auth.denied` / 401 and the twenty-first writes `mcp.auth.ratelimited` / 429. That suite is the one that binds the documented budget to the code that spends it: the limiter's own test file mirrors the call pair instead of importing the middleware, so it cannot see a call-site regression by itself. **Evidence.** 1852 passed / 93 skipped / 0 failed on this branch against 1851 / 93 / 0 on `umbrella`, `tsc --noEmit` clean. Mutation-checked three ways against the audit suite: restore `isRateLimited` at the three sites, 1 red; keep `isCurrentlyLimited` but record BEFORE the check, 1 red; halve the constructor to 10, 3 red across both files. The prerequisite is that upstream's limiter has no read-only accessor at all — `isCurrentlyLimited` is a fork addition (`c0e3cad`) — so a port carries that method with it. |

## How to update the fork against upstream

```bash
git fetch upstream
git checkout main
git rebase upstream/main
git push origin main

# Now reconcile umbrella against the new main.
git checkout umbrella
git rebase main
# Resolve conflicts. Drop any of our cherry-picks that upstream has merged
# (the cherry-pick log above tells you which).
git push --force-with-lease origin umbrella
```

Cadence: monthly minimum, or whenever a real upstream commit lands that we want.

## Build + deploy

`umbrella` is built by `.github/workflows/umbrella-build.yml`. Push events to `umbrella` build **and publish** — login, tag `ghcr.io/umbrella-it-group/metamcp:<sha>` + `:latest`, push, and attest all run. Pull-request events into `umbrella` run the same job as a **check only**: `docker/login-action`, `docker/metadata-action`, and `attest-build-provenance` are skipped, `build-push-action` runs with `push: true` only on the push path (`push: false` on PRs), proving the Dockerfile still builds without publishing or touching the registry. (This PR-check path only exists as of PR #63, 2026-07-13 — before that fix the job's `if` matched `push` only, so the `pull_request` trigger existed but silently never fired; see the cherry-pick log below.) Pinned in the deploy host's compose fragment. Watchtower (with `scope=umbrella` label) auto-updates the running container within ~5 min of an image push.

To deploy a hotfix:

1. Branch off `umbrella`, commit, push, open PR against `umbrella`, get review, squash-merge.
2. CI builds `:latest` automatically.
3. Watchtower polls every 5 min; container restart picks up the new image.
4. If you need it instantly, run `docker compose up -d --no-deps metamcp` on the deploy host.

### Repo visibility (operator standing decision, 2026-07-21)

This repo is **PUBLIC**, deliberately. GitHub Actions minutes are billed
per-job on private repos but free on public ones — flipping to public on
2026-07-21 took this fork's ~253 min/mo of Actions usage to $0. Flipping
back to private re-arms roughly that same ~250-400 min/mo of billing (the
range accounts for cherry-pick/dependency-sweep months running heavier
than quiet months). Any future call to make this repo private again should
weigh that cost explicitly, not flip it as a routine hygiene pass — check
here first.

## Upstreaming our work

When we write a patch that's not Umbrella-specific (env-var fallbacks, bug fixes, etc.):

1. Branch off `main` (not `umbrella`) so the patch is clean against upstream.
2. Open PR against `metatool-ai/metamcp`.
3. Once merged upstream, drop our private cherry-pick during the next rebase.

The cherry-pick log doubles as our own upstream-PR backlog — anything in "Our own patches" is a candidate.

## Deferred / future work

- **Port the SDK 1.30.0 stdio ReadBuffer 10MB cap into the fork's vendored copy** (`apps/backend/src/lib/stdio-transport/shared.ts`, uncapped `append()`): the pool fronts STDIO backends whose output we do not control, and the vendored transport bypasses the SDK cap entirely. Surfaced by the #95 review 2026-08-13.
- **Scope `namespaces.refreshTools`'s server lookup to the servers mapped into the namespace being refreshed** (`apps/backend/src/trpc/namespaces.impl.ts`, `mcpServersRepository.findByName`). Fork PR #114 closed tool poisoning through this path for PUBLIC namespaces only, by requiring the admin role when `user_id` is NULL. A namespace OWNER still reaches the writes on their own namespace, which is deliberate, but the write is not confined to that namespace: the caller-supplied tool name carries its own `ServerName__toolName` prefix, and `findByName` matches that prefix against the ENTIRE `mcp_servers` table with no user or namespace predicate (`apps/backend/src/db/repositories/mcp-servers.repo.ts`). So an owner, including an admin who was later demoted but kept a namespace they created, can overwrite `description` and `tool_schema` on any server in the estate by naming it, and downstream MCP clients are then told whatever that description says. Follow-up: resolve the server from the namespace's own mappings (`namespaceMappingsRepository`) rather than by global name, and skip any tool whose prefix names a server outside `input.namespaceUuid`. Surfaced by the #114 review 2026-08-15.
### MCP spec 2026-07-28 catch-up (the "sessionless rewrite"), parked 2026-08-13

Source: a research pass that read the MCP spec revision dated 2026-07-28 end to end against this fork. Ruling: park Phase 2-3 with the ideas in the repo, revisit at a later date, after confirming the surrounding systems will support it. Two phases, filed as the paired counterpart to an internal backlog entry ("MetaMCP fork backlog") where the full writeup lives. This is the pointer, not a duplicate; read that entry before starting anything below.

**Phase 2, gated on three named triggers (verify each, then re-verify the operator's own systems-support gate before starting):**

1. TypeScript `@modelcontextprotocol/sdk` v2 goes STABLE. Currently 2.0.0 beta, itself a package split (core/client/server/...) rather than a drop-in bump. This fork pins v1 today: `apps/backend/package.json` and `apps/frontend/package.json` both carry `1.30.0` (the v1 line was established by fork PR #64's zod v4 migration at `1.29.0`, currency-bumped by PR #95).
2. `fastmcp` 4.0 goes STABLE. Currently 4.0.0b2. The 4.x line serves sessionless alongside the handshake era (dual support), which is the property every backend MCP depends on existing before this bump touches anything.
3. claude.ai connectors actually negotiate protocol version 2026-07-28. Per Anthropic's docs fetched 2026-08-13, connector support today goes only through 2025-11-25.

Key facts a 2027 resumer needs on this fork specifically:

- The gateway must stay dual-era (serving both the pre-rewrite handshake protocol and the new sessionless one) for as long as any Claude surface we serve, meaning every claude.ai connector today, still speaks handshake-era. No flag day exists in the spec that forces a cutover.
- This fork carries zero hardcoded `protocolVersion` anywhere; the SDK negotiates it. That removes one migration step but not the one below.
- `apps/backend/src/lib/metamcp/transport-recovery-hydration.ts` is the number one migration risk. It reaches into SDK-private internals to hydrate lazily-recovered StreamableHTTP transports (the fix that made PRs #15/#22/#23/#24 actually work). Any SDK major bump needs this file re-verified line by line against the new internals before merge; do not assume compatibility.
- Backends across the platform are pure tools-over-POST (205 tools fleet-wide, no sampling, no elicitation, no resources), so the migration's real risk concentrates in this gateway's session/transport plumbing, not in the per-vendor MCP servers it fronts.
- No deprecation clock forces this migration. Legacy servers keep serving dual-era clients indefinitely under the spec. The 12-month deprecation runway announced 2026-07-28 covers only HTTP+SSE transport, the Roots / Sampling / Logging capabilities, and RFC 7591 Dynamic Client Registration (see Phase 3 below), none of which is `protocolVersion` itself.

**Canary path, not a fresh design question.** Per the operator's standing 2026-08-04 ruling (`Exploitacious/Personal-MCP-Server` `README.md:185-188`), the personal MCP instance is the designated fork canary for this exact upgrade: prove it there first, single-operator, low blast radius, before this fork or any Umbrella production backend touches it.

**Phase 3, opportunistic, no date, pick up individually:**

- **Tasks extension (`io.modelcontextprotocol/tasks`).** Rolling out across Claude products. Watch for it reaching a stable client surface before scoping gateway support.
- **Elicitation.** Client-gated, asymmetrically, today: Claude Code supports form and URL modes; claude.ai connectors do not. Revisit when claude.ai adds support, not before.
- **MCP Apps.** No current consumer need identified. Track only.
- **CIMD migration off deprecated RFC 7591 DCR.** claude.ai still uses DCR today; keep both until Anthropic actually moves claude.ai off it. This is the one item on an actual clock: a 12-month deprecation runway was announced 2026-07-28.
- **Enterprise Managed Auth extension.** No current consumer need identified. Track only.

Discovery evidence and the full research trail live in internal notes, not copied into this repo. Pull those before re-deriving any of the above from scratch.

The `LICENSE` file is unchanged from upstream (MIT). Upstream copyright notice is preserved. Our fork-specific changes are clearly marked in commit messages and in this file's logs.
| 2026-07-10 | [#44](https://github.com/Umbrella-IT-Group/metamcp/pull/44) | **M365 delegated-token broker** — the gateway-side linchpin of the per-user Microsoft 365 MCP program (design doc: internal M365 delegated-MCP design, §4-5). Four surfaces. (1) *Enrollment routes* `GET /m365/enroll` → Entra auth-code+PKCE → `GET /m365/callback` (+ `/m365/status`, `POST /m365/disconnect`, TEMP `GET /m365/me` Graph echo), better-auth session-gated, state⇄user bound (callback requires the SAME signed-in user that started the flow); `next.config.js` gains the `/m365/:path*` rewrite (same requirement as `/health`). (2) *Token custody* — new `m365_user_tokens` table (migration `0018`, idempotent) holding AES-256-GCM envelopes only (`lib/m365/crypto.ts`, KEK from the SOPS-vaulted `M365_TOKEN_KEK`, AAD-bound header, per-envelope `kek_id` for rotation); deliberately NOT better-auth's plaintext `accounts` table; row cascades with user deletion. (3) *Mint service* (`lib/m365/mint-service.ts`) — per-user AT cache (60s expiry buffer), per-user single-flight (Entra rotates RTs on redemption; concurrent redemption kills the grant), atomic rotate-and-persist with in-memory pending-rotation recovery when the persist write fails, typed failure taxonomy (`credential_missing/expired/revoked`, `mfa_required`, `not_configured`, `mint_failed`) mapped from Entra error_codes, one JSON audit line per mint (no token material). DB-free module graph via type-only import + lazy repo resolution (same doctrine as `consumer-identity-resolver.ts`). (4) *Per-request injection* — `streamable-http.ts` wraps all three `transport.handleRequest` dispatch sites in an `AsyncLocalStorage` user context (OAuth consumers only); `client.ts` passes a custom transport `fetch` (`lib/m365/injected-fetch.ts`) for servers named in `M365_INJECTED_SERVER_NAMES` (default `m365`) that strips any inbound credential and stamps `Authorization: Bearer <minted Graph AT>` + `X-Umbrella-User-Id` per REQUEST. Request-scoped injection is the only design that survives the pool's idle-warmup handoff, per-server cap-reuse and global params cache without cross-user token leakage — connect-time headers are provably unsafe here. No context (idle warmup, health ping, API-key consumer, OpenAPI bridge) → NO Authorization header at all, fail-closed. `metamcp-proxy.ts` catches typed broker errors in the tools/call handler → structured `isError` result carrying the enroll URL + best-effort SEP-1036 URL-mode elicitation (fire-and-forget; SDK 1.16 predates URL mode so it's sent raw and swallowed on rejection). Boots CLEANLY with no M365 env (routes answer 503 `not_configured`, injection inert) so the image deploys before the Entra app exists. 38 new vitest cases across crypto (tamper/AAD/wrong-KEK), mint (single-flight, rotation-persist-failure recovery, error taxonomy), the injection invariant (never forwards inbound credential, no-context → no header, typed propagation, ALS through async hops) and the error→result mapping, plus review-driven additions — 14 route-level integration tests on the enrollment surface (XSS escaping + no-script CSP, single-use state, session⇄state anti-grafting binding, envelope-not-plaintext custody, PKCE verifier passthrough) and 4 transport-wiring cases pinning byte-identical construction for non-injected servers; 222 suite-wide. Fix: the job now runs on both events; only the registry-touching steps (`docker/login-action`, `docker/metadata-action`, `attest-build-provenance`, and `push: true` on `build-push-action`) stay push-gated via their own `if`, so a PR run proves the Dockerfile still builds without publishing anything. `paths:` filtering unchanged for both triggers. Verified live on itself: `gh pr checks 63` → `build-and-push pass 2m27s`; the job's step list confirms `docker/login-action`/`docker/metadata-action`/`attest-build-provenance` all show skipped (`-`) while checkout/buildx/build-push ran and passed. `actionlint` clean before and after. `gh pr view 63` → `"mergeable":"MERGEABLE","mergeStateStatus":"CLEAN"`. Note for the operator: the first PR-triggered build has no `cache-to` write (by design — a PR build never writes the shared `metamcp-umbrella` GHA cache scope it doesn't own), so it may run slower than a push-triggered build; later PR builds still get `cache-from` reads against whatever the push path already wrote. `workflow_dispatch` was already unreachable before this change too (same job-level `if`) — untouched here, flagged as a separate follow-up if manual dispatch is meant to work. | 2026-07-13 | [#62](https://github.com/Umbrella-IT-Group/metamcp/pull/62) | **Per-server "Reconnect Server" — flushes the pooled tool list without a gateway restart (parts 1+2 of the no-restart refresh brief; part 3 deferred, see below)** — MetaMCP re-lists tools live on every `tools/list`, but over one upstream connection per server the pool keeps warm forever; idle sessions are only health-pinged, never recycled while healthy, so an upstream rename/add keeps serving its connect-time list indefinitely (the DB resync is additionally gated behind `toolsSyncCache.hasChanged`, which never trips on a live rename). New "Reconnect Server" button (confirm dialog + toast) beside Refresh Tools on the single-server tool-management surface; Refresh Tools itself is left as-is (it re-lists over the SAME pooled connection, so it cannot bust rename-rot — the new button is the actual flush). Owner-or-public trust boundary matches update/delete. `pnpm run build` exit 0 (4/4 turbo tasks); backend vitest **236 passed, 19 files** (7 new `mcp-servers.reconnect.impl.test.ts` cases: full-cascade fan-out, no-namespace path, public-server allow, foreign-owner deny + no cascade, not-found + no cascade, primary-cascade failure surfaced not swallowed, namespace fan-out best-effort). **Part 3 (auto-detect via a timer-driven idle-connection re-list + hash-compare, no manual click) is DEFERRED, not shipped** — binding constraint: the stated failure mechanism is that the warm pooled connection serves its connect-time tool list; if that is literally true, re-listing over the SAME idle connection returns the same stale list, `toolsSyncCache`'s hash never changes, and the auto-detector is a no-op against the exact problem it targets. Idle sessions also have no `list_changed` subscribers (they attach on `getSession`), so an auto-fired `invalidateServerConnection` against a purely-idle server has no one to fan out to. Falsifier: a live experiment where an upstream renames a tool WITHOUT dropping its connection, and a fresh `tools/list` over the retained idle connection returns the new name, would confirm the detector can work — needs a real upstream on the live gateway, out of scope for this lane. Parts 1+2 fully kill the manual-restart ritual for a given server, which is the lane's core DONE criterion. | 2026-07-13 | [#61](https://github.com/Umbrella-IT-Group/metamcp/pull/61) | **Bounded warmup fallback for `tools/call` reconnect-window 404** — closes a routing gap the invalidate-and-retry cascade (PR #13/#16/#28) doesn't cover: `McpServerPool.invalidateServerConnection` tears the pooled client down and fires `list_changed` **before** the replacement connection exists, and a call landing in that gap falls through the dynamic-find loop's `if (session)` guard without the owning server's name ever being checked — `clientForTool` stays unset, the handler throws `"Unknown tool: ..."`, and the OpenAPI bridge maps that to a literal HTTP 404 for a tool that is real (the transient 404 the n8n pipeline already retry-hardens around). Tools that never existed anywhere skip the wait entirely (zero added latency); a server removed from the namespace has no `params` to open a session with (also zero added latency); a genuinely dead but still-member upstream is bounded at ~10s, never an unbounded hang. `pnpm run build` exit 0 (4/4 turbo tasks); `check-types` exit 0; backend `tsc --noEmit` 0 errors (no `check-types` script on `apps/backend`, run directly since it's the live gateway); backend vitest **234 passed, 19 files** (5 new `tool-call-warmup.test.ts` cases: fast-path resolve, bounded retry, bounded exhaustion returns `undefined` without hanging, custom `attempts` override, defensive reject-handling). `pnpm run lint` (`--max-warnings 0`) fails at 36 pre-existing backend warnings verified identical against untouched `origin/umbrella` (none in a file this PR touches) — not fixed, out of scope for this lane. Matches this file's own `Janhouse/metamcp` note ("zod 3→4, MCP SDK 1.16→1.29 — conflict-heavy, take last"); the SDK bump needs that migration as a prerequisite. (1) New `users.role` (`text NOT NULL default 'member'`, `'admin' | 'member'`; migration `0020`, hand-written idempotent per fork convention, seeds the bootstrap operator account → `admin` via idempotent `UPDATE` by email — **fresh-install note**: on a virgin database the seed UPDATE matches zero rows, so NO ADMIN EXISTS until someone is promoted manually after sign-up; an existing deployment is unaffected, that row already exists there) surfaced into the better-auth session via `user.additionalFields` with `input: false` (no self-escalation; sessions read fresh from DB per request so demotion is immediate). (2) `adminProcedure` = `protectedProcedure` + `role==='admin'` (FORBIDDEN otherwise; unauth still UNAUTHORIZED first) plus an extracted pure `requireAdmin` guard, gating: create/update/delete of MCP servers, namespaces, endpoints; the three namespace **curation** mutations `updateServerStatus`/`updateToolStatus`/`updateToolOverrides` (same "destructive update" class — they change which servers/tools an agent sees); `tools.create`/`tools.sync` (writes to the shared tools catalog); `logs.clear`; `oauth.upsert` (upstream MCP-server OAuth credential writes); the entire global config write surface (`setSignupDisabled`/`setSsoSignupDisabled`/`setBasicAuthDisabled`/`setSessionLifetime`/`setConfig`/the MCP timeout+attempt setters — reads keep their existing access level); the api-key admin listing; and public-key minting. `mcpServers.bulkImport` gated too (a create path that would otherwise bypass the create gate). Deliberately LEFT member-accessible (operational nudges, no config mutation, each commented in-line so a future sweep doesn't "fix" them): `namespaces.refreshTools` and `mcpServers.reconnect` — verified by code inspection that `refreshTools` calls `toolsRepository.bulkUpsert` directly from `namespaces.impl.ts`, never routing through the now-gated `tools.create`/`tools.sync` procedures, so gating those has no effect on it. Members keep read surfaces + minting **private** keys + managing their own; the public MCP request path + better-auth flows are untouched. (3) New nullable `api_keys.last_used_at` (migration `0021`) stamped fire-and-forget + throttled to 15 min by `validateApiKey` (pure decision in `api-keys.last-used.ts`; never awaited, never rejects — the hot public-endpoint auth path gains no latency and no failure mode; existing n8n/Claude/other consumers behavior-identical bar the additive stamp). (4) Dormant `findAll()` wired into an admin-only `listAll` tRPC query (LEFT JOIN users → owner email or `null`=public, created, last-used, active) that returns a non-reversible key **prefix only**, never the raw secret — deliberately different from the member-facing `list`, which continues returning the FULL key value for public ('everyone') keys (that's the "copy the shared key to configure your client" feature; a public key is already distributed to every consumer, so masking it there would only reduce usefulness, not exposure — private keys are never exposed this way). (5) `updateAsAdmin`/`deleteAsAdmin` bypass the ownership WHERE so admins revoke/disable/delete ANY key including public ones; members stay owner-scoped. **BLOCKER fix from independent security review (2026-07-14, same day)**: the member-scoped `update()`/`delete()` WHERE originally read `or(eq(user_id, userId), isNull(user_id))` — the `isNull` branch also matched PUBLIC keys, so any member holding a public key's uuid (visible via their own `list`) could deactivate or DELETE it. Narrowed to `eq(user_id, userId)` only; a public key is now mutable exclusively through `updateAsAdmin`/`deleteAsAdmin`. Proven with a structural regression test that spies on drizzle-orm's real `or`/`isNull` and asserts neither is invoked by the member path. Mint path also rejects a member assigning a key to another user's id (ownership spoofing), not just the public case. (6) Frontend API Keys page reads the session role → admin-only "All API Keys" section (owner/created/last-used/active/revoke, sourced from the gated `listAll` which members never fire) + hides the 'everyone' mint option for non-admins; no separate admin nav route (the role-gated section is the admin surface). | 2026-07-14 | [#70](https://github.com/Umbrella-IT-Group/metamcp/pull/70) | **Periodic full-definition tools/list sweep — no-reboot tool updates (no-reboot wave, Track A2)** — the pull half of the no-reboot pair. Prod backends deliver tool updates as container REPLACES that kill the process (fastmcp `add_tool`/`remove_tool` have no notify hook); on replace the SDK standalone GET stream — the only push channel — dies and its auto-reconnect exhausts before the replacement container boots, so no `tools/list_changed` push survives an update. This adds a pool-level periodic PULL sweep in `mcp-server-pool.ts`: every `TOOLS_SWEEP_INTERVAL_SECONDS` (env, default 60, `0` disables — no timer scheduled), for each backend that ALREADY has a live pooled connection (active preferred since those carry the `listChangedSubscribers`; idle is a valid detection fallback — never opens a NEW connection just to sweep), it re-lists tools over that existing connection (paginated to mirror `metamcp-proxy`'s loop), computes the Track A3 full-definition hash. On drift it logs one INFO line (`tool definitions changed for <server>, resyncing`) and runs the SAME cascade a transport drop uses — `invalidateServerConnection` → PR #19 `list_changed` fan-out → consumer re-list → DB resync — with NO new propagation mechanism. **Sweep-owned baseline (added in the review round, see below):** the sweep compares against a baseline it owns itself (`toolsSweepLastHash`, per serverUuid) — NOT `toolsSyncCache`. Seeds silently on first observation, updates on every tick, invalidates only when the new hash differs from the sweep's OWN prior observation — `toolsSyncCache` is used only as a pure hashing utility (`hashTools`), never its stateful `hasChanged`/`update` surface. This makes the invalidate fire exactly once per real change regardless of whether anything downstream reacts. Guard rails: one sweep in flight at a time (overlapping tick skipped); per-server failures (tools/list OR the invalidate cascade) logged at debug and never abort the loop or crash the timer; bounded 10s per-request timeout; a non-advancing `nextCursor` stops pagination immediately (WARN) and `TOOLS_SWEEP_MAX_PAGES` (50) backstops any other infinite-pagination shape (WARN); timer cleared on pool dispose (`cleanupAll`); `toolsSweepLastHash` pruned on server deregistration (same category as the existing `lastErrorProbeAt`/`activePingFailures` cleanup); runs as an ordinary JSON-RPC request over the shared transport so it multiplexes with in-flight tool calls. `TOOLS_SWEEP_INTERVAL_SECONDS` added to `turbo.json`'s env allowlist. **Review round (2026-07-14, verdict MERGE-AFTER-FIXES):** three findings on the first cut, which compared the sweep directly against `toolsSyncCache`. Finding 1 (high): an idle-only server has zero `listChangedSubscribers`, so the fan-out reaches no one and `toolsSyncCache` never updates downstream — comparing against that frozen cache re-detected the SAME already-handled change every ~60s tick FOREVER (invalidate → health-check timer recreates the idle connection → re-detect), on exactly the Watchtower-replace scenario this PR targets. Finding 2 (medium): `tools.impl.sync` overwrites `toolsSyncCache` with the namespace-FILTERED tool set whenever `filterOutOverrideTools` drops a tool, while the sweep always lists the UNFILTERED set — permanent false-drift even for servers with active consumers. Fix (closes 1+2): the sweep-owned baseline described above — owning a separate map makes both failure modes structurally impossible. Finding 3 (low): `listToolsForSweep`'s pagination had no cursor-advance guard; a backend returning a constant `nextCursor` would loop forever, and since `toolsSweepInProgress` only clears in the sweep's `finally`, that would wedge the sweep permanently. Fix: the non-advancing-cursor + page-cap guards described above. `mcp-server-pool.test.ts` sweep suite: 10 cases (rewritten for two-tick seed-then-observe semantics) incl. the three review-requested regressions — (a) multi-tick idle-only/zero-subscribers: one real change → exactly ONE invalidate, quiet after; (b) baseline independence: a stale/filtered `toolsSyncCache` entry doesn't fire when the listed unfiltered set is unchanged across ticks; (c) non-advancing cursor trips the guard and the in-flight lock releases for the next tick — plus a hard-page-cap test. Gates: backend vitest **260 passed (20 files)**, backend `tsc --noEmit` exit 0, `pnpm run build` 4/4 turbo tasks, ESLint `--max-warnings 0` introduces zero new warnings on touched files (the 3 remaining `no-explicit-any` on `backgroundIdleSessionsByNamespace` are pre-existing on `umbrella`, verified). **Base: `feat-tools-fulldef-hash` (PR #69), NOT `umbrella` — stacked; MERGE AFTER #69** and do not squash-merge #69 with `--delete-branch` while this PR still targets it (auto-closes it): retarget to `umbrella` after #69 lands, or merge #69 first without deleting its branch. | 2026-07-14 | [#71](https://github.com/Umbrella-IT-Group/metamcp/pull/71) | **Non-retryable m365 broker errors on cold connect + `closing` guard + actionable connect-error logs (Track A5).** Fixes three cold-connect defects in `connectMetaMcpClient`, all confirmed against prod logs. (1) The per-user m365 broker mints a Graph token on EVERY request including the backend `initialize` handshake, so a fresh connect for an unlinked user throws `M365BrokerError` (`credential_missing`) — a deterministic identity rejection. The retry loop caught it as any error and retried 3× (the "Connect attempt N/3 failed" storm) then resolved `undefined`; the enrollment elicitation (`buildM365BrokerErrorResult`) only ran on the WARM tool-call path, so a cold-connecting unlinked user never saw the enroll URL (a teammate hit exactly this). Now: detect `isM365BrokerError` in the catch → one attempt, no retry, single INFO line (identity state, not a server fault). **Enrollment delivery mechanism (the design-latitude call):** the cold connect runs inside the consumer's request ALS context (`runWithM365UserContext` wraps `transport.handleRequest`), so the broker error is user-attributable — but it can't simply be re-thrown: the pool's connect path swallows it to `undefined` (`mcp-server-pool.ts` is off-limits, owned by another lane) and the proxy's dynamic-find catch-and-continue must keep routing non-m365 tools. So connect LATCHES the error into a mutable field on the request-scoped `M365UserContext` (`recordConnectBrokerFailure`, `lib/m365/request-context.ts`); the outer `tools/call` handler in `metamcp-proxy.ts` — the same handler that already translates warm-path broker errors — drains it (`takeConnectBrokerFailure`) when the call otherwise failed and answers with `buildM365BrokerErrorResult` (structured `isError` + enroll_url + SEP-1036 elicitation), the SAME surface as the warm path. Delivery is gated on the requested tool's prefix matching the latched server name, so a stray m365 probe while routing an unrelated tool can't hijack that tool's error. Pool contract unchanged (connect still resolves `undefined`) — zero blast radius outside three files. `tools/list` cleanly omits the unconnectable backend (can't carry an `isError` tool result); the prompt reaches the user on tool invocation. (2) The failed-connect cleanup closed the transport WITHOUT setting the `closing` guard, logging its own intentional close as "Transport closed unexpectedly (backend drop)" twice per attempt — now `closing` is hoisted to loop scope and set in the catch, and the drop handlers additionally require `connectedAt !== undefined` (handshake completed) before logging a drop, so a never-established transport is a connect-time failure (surfaced by the connect-attempt log), not a fake drop; real established-then-dropped detection (watchtower/container-replace) preserved, now stamped with connection age (`established 2h ago` vs `never-connected`). (3) New `lib/metamcp/connect-error.ts` recursively unwraps undici's nested `.cause` and `AggregateError.errors` (cycle-safe) to the leaf syscall/code/address — logs `connect ECONNREFUSED 172.18.0.13:3000` instead of `fetch failed`/`terminated` — reused at the connect-failure log and both transport drop-log sites; no new INFO lines for healthy operation. Tests: `connect-error.test.ts` (undici nested cause, AggregateError, plain/non-Error, cycles, age), `request-context.test.ts` (latch record/take, fail-open, no cross-request leak, end-to-end same-as-warm enrollment result), `client-connect.test.ts` (broker → one attempt + latch + no drop/attempt logs + no `onTransportDrop`; transient → 3 retries + unwrapped cause; closing guard both directions). Gates: backend `vitest` **318 passed, 30 files**, backend `tsc --noEmit` 0 errors, `eslint --max-warnings 0` clean on touched files, backend `tsup` build clean (full `pnpm build` 4/4 verified pre-rebase; diff is backend-only). Branched off `origin/umbrella`, rebased onto current `umbrella` (incl. #68/#69/#70) — clean 3-way. `mcp-server-pool.ts` and `tools-sync-cache.ts` untouched.
